* **Anatoly A. Kilbas, Hari M. Srivastava, and Juan J. Trujillo, *Theory and
  Applications of Fractional Differential Equations*** (2006,
  [Elsevier](https://doi.org/10.1016/s0304-0208(06)x8001-5), North-Holland
  Mathematics Studies). The standard reference for fractional integrals and
  derivatives and the equations built from them.
* **René L. Schilling, Renming Song, and Zoran Vondraček, *Bernstein
  Functions***. Complete monotonicity and its many appearances across
  analysis and probability.
* **I. I. Hirschman and D. V. Widder, *The Convolution Transform***.
  Inversion of convolution transforms by differential operators.

### More recent works

* **NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions**, and **Frank
  W. J. Olver, Daniel W. Lozier, Ronald F. Boisvert, and Charles
  W. Clark (eds.), *NIST Handbook of Mathematical Functions*** (2010,
  Cambridge University Press). The modern authoritative reference for
  definitions, identities, and asymptotics; the first place to look.
* **Richard Beals and Roderick Wong, *Special Functions and Orthogonal
  Polynomials*** (2016, Cambridge University Press). A modern rigorous
  graduate text.
* **Carlo Viola, *An Introduction to Special Functions*** (2016,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-41345-7)). A graduate course on
  gamma, hypergeometric, Bessel, and related functions. Useful for learning
  the analytic theory behind the reference tables.
* **Mourad E. H. Ismail (ed., with assistance from Walter Van Assche); Tom H.
  Koornwinder and Jasper V. Stokman (eds., volume II), *Encyclopedia of
  Special Functions: The Askey–Bateman Project*** (volumes I–II, 2020–2021:
  *Univariate Orthogonal Polynomials*; *Multivariable Special Functions*,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9780511979156); [volume II](https://doi.org/10.1017/9780511777165)). A
  modern extension of the Bateman project, covering classical and basic
  orthogonal polynomials and multivariable functions. The two published
  volumes are cited together as a reference work.
* **Roelof Koekoek, Peter A. Lesky, and René F. Swarttouw,
  *Hypergeometric Orthogonal Polynomials and Their q-Analogues***
  (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-05014-5)). Listed in Section 48.
* **Victor H. Moll, *Special Integrals of Gradshteyn and Ryzhik: The
  Proofs*** (2 volumes, 2014–2015, [CRC
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1201/b17674); [volume II](https://www.routledge.com/Special-Integrals-of-Gradshteyn-and-Ryzhik-the-Proofs---Volume-II/Moll/p/book/9780367377274)). Proofs of entries in the table,
  organised by method.
* **Takashi Takebe, *Elliptic Integrals and Elliptic Functions*** (2023,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-30265-7)). Elliptic integrals,
  elliptic functions, and their analytic relations. A recent graduate
  treatment of a classical subject that connects function theory with
  integrable systems.
* **Ranjan Roy, *Elliptic and Modular Functions from Gauss to Dedekind
  to Hecke*** (2017, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781316671504)). Listed in Section 2.
* **George Dassios, *Ellipsoidal Harmonics: Theory and Applications*** (2012,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139017749)).
  Lamé functions, ellipsoidal harmonics, and boundary-value problems in
  ellipsoidal geometry. A research reference for separation of variables
  beyond spherical coordinates.
* **Yoshishige Haraoka, *Linear Differential Equations in the Complex
  Domain: From Classical Theory to Forefront*** (2020,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-54663-2), Lecture Notes in
  Mathematics). Fuchsian equations, monodromy, rigidity, and middle
  convolution.
* **Rudolf Gorenflo, Anatoly A. Kilbas, Francesco Mainardi, and Sergei
  V. Rogosin, *Mittag-Leffler Functions, Related Topics and
  Applications*** (2nd ed., 2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-61550-8)). The monograph on
  Mittag-Leffler functions and their role in fractional equations.
* **Kai Diethelm, *The Analysis of Fractional Differential Equations:
  An Application-Oriented Exposition Using Differential Operators of
  Caputo Type*** (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-14574-2)). Existence, regularity, and the
  special functions of fractional differential equations. A large
  applied literature surrounds this subject; this is one of the
  careful analytic accounts.

---

## 50. Asymptotic Analysis, Resurgence, and Perturbation

Asymptotic expansions of integrals and of solutions of differential
equations, Borel summability and resurgence, singular perturbations, and
spectral asymptotics. The more recent books continue the Écalle school and
exact WKB theory, and provide further courses on singular perturbations and
differential-equation asymptotics.

### Foundational works

#### Asymptotic expansions and integrals

* **F. W. J. Olver, *Asymptotics and Special Functions***. The
  foundational modern reference for asymptotic expansions, turning
  points, uniform asymptotics, and error bounds.
* **R. Wong, *Asymptotic Approximations of Integrals***. The standard
  detailed reference for Laplace, stationary phase, saddle points, and
  uniform methods.
* **N. G. de Bruijn, *Asymptotic Methods in Analysis***. A short
  classic on Laplace's method, saddle points, and implicit
  asymptotics.
* **Norman Bleistein and Richard A. Handelsman, *Asymptotic Expansions
  of Integrals***. A practical treatment of steepest descent and
  uniform approximation.
* **A. Erdélyi, *Asymptotic Expansions***. A short classical
  introduction to asymptotic expansions.
* **E. T. Copson, *Asymptotic Expansions***. A compact classical
  treatment, with the expansion of integrals done in detail.
* **R. B. Paris and D. Kaminski, *Asymptotics and Mellin–Barnes
  Integrals***. Mellin methods, with exponentially improved
  expansions.
* **R. B. Dingle, *Asymptotic Expansions: Their Derivation and
  Interpretation***. The source of the modern language of late terms
  and Stokes smoothing.
* **Philippe Flajolet and Robert Sedgewick, *Analytic Combinatorics***
  (2009, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511801655)). Singularity analysis,
  saddle points, and Mellin transforms applied to generating functions.
  Complex analysis as a counting tool.
* **V. I. Arnold, S. M. Gusein-Zade, and A. N. Varchenko, *Singularities of
  Differentiable Maps, Volume II: Monodromy and Asymptotics of
  Integrals***. Asymptotics of oscillatory integrals via singularity
  theory.
* **Jacob Korevaar, *Tauberian Theory: A Century of
  Developments***. Tauberian theorems from Hardy–Littlewood and Wiener to
  modern applications.

#### Differential equations, summability, and resurgence

* **M. V. Fedoryuk, *Asymptotic Analysis: Linear Ordinary Differential
  Equations*** and his work on the saddle-point method. The
  Russian-school treatment.
* **Wolfgang Wasow, *Asymptotic Expansions for Ordinary Differential
  Equations***. The classical reference for irregular singular points
  and turning-point theory.
* **Roscoe B. White, *Asymptotic Analysis of Differential Equations***
  (revised ed., 2010, [Imperial College Press](https://doi.org/10.1142/p735);
  first edition 2005). WKB methods, turning points, boundary layers, and
  complex asymptotic matching. Useful for connecting formal
  differential-equation approximations with integral representations.
* **Ovidiu Costin, *Asymptotics and Borel Summability***. The standard
  book-length treatment of resurgence and Borel summation for
  analysts.
* **Werner Balser, *Formal Power Series and Linear Systems of
  Meromorphic Ordinary Differential Equations***. Multisummability and
  the Stokes phenomenon, rigorously.
* **Takahiro Kawai and Yoshitsugu Takei, *Algebraic Analysis of Singular
  Perturbation Theory*** (2005, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/mmono/227), Translations of Mathematical
  Monographs). Exact WKB analysis, Borel resummation, and the Painlevé
  equations.
* **Robert Conte and Micheline Musette, *The Painlevé Handbook*** (2nd
  ed., 2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-53340-3); first
  edition 2008). A handbook of Painlevé equations, their solutions, and
  their asymptotics.
* **Peter D. Miller, *Applied Asymptotic Analysis***. A modern
  graduate course including a careful introduction to the
  Riemann–Hilbert method.
* **Wolfgang Wasow, *Linear Turning Point Theory***. Uniform asymptotics
  for linear differential equations with turning points.

#### Perturbation methods and averaging

* **Carl M. Bender and Steven A. Orszag, *Advanced Mathematical
  Methods for Scientists and Engineers***. Formal asymptotics, WKB,
  boundary layers, and matched expansions; less rigorous than the
  rest, and exceptionally influential.
* **Ali H. Nayfeh, *Perturbation Methods***. Multiple scales, matched
  asymptotics, and the standard applied perturbation methods.
* **J. Kevorkian and J. D. Cole, *Multiple Scale and Singular
  Perturbation Methods***. The systematic treatment of multiple-scale
  and singular perturbation methods.
* **Jan A. Sanders, Ferdinand Verhulst, and James Murdock, *Averaging
  Methods in Nonlinear Dynamical Systems*** (2nd ed., 2007, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-48918-6)).
  Listed in Section 31. The rigorous theory of averaging.
* **Robert E. O'Malley Jr., *Singular Perturbation Methods for Ordinary
  Differential Equations***. Boundary layers and matched expansions for
  ODE.
* **P. A. Lagerstrom, *Matched Asymptotic Expansions: Ideas and
  Techniques***. The conceptual foundations of matched asymptotic
  expansions.

#### Spectral asymptotics

* **Mouez Dimassi and Johannes Sjöstrand, *Spectral Asymptotics in the
  Semi-Classical Limit***. Asymptotics as spectral theory; see also
  Section 30.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis and Precise Spectral
  Asymptotics***. The definitive treatment of sharp Weyl asymptotics.

### More recent works

* **Nico M. Temme, *Asymptotic Methods for Integrals*** (2015, [World
  Scientific](https://doi.org/10.1142/9195)). Saddle points, uniform
  expansions, and the asymptotics of special functions, in the tradition of
  the DLMF.
* **R. B. Paris, *Hadamard Expansions and Hyperasymptotic Evaluation: An
  Extension of the Method of Steepest Descents*** (2011, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511753626)). Convergent
  Hadamard expansions and exponentially improved asymptotics.
* **Claude Mitschi and David Sauzin, *Divergent Series, Summability and
  Resurgence I*** (2016, Springer, Lecture Notes in Mathematics). The most
  accessible route into Écalle's theory.
* **Michèle Loday-Richaud, *Divergent Series, Summability and
  Resurgence II: Simple and Multiple Summability*** (2016, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-29075-1),
  Lecture Notes in Mathematics). The second volume of the series whose
  first volume is by Mitschi and Sauzin.
* **Eric Delabaere, *Divergent Series, Summability and Resurgence III:
  Resurgent Methods and the First Painlevé Equation*** (2016, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-29000-3),
  Lecture Notes in Mathematics). Resurgence applied to Painlevé I.
* **Claude Sabbah, *Introduction to Stokes Structures*** (2013,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-31695-1), Lecture Notes in Mathematics). Stokes filtrations and the
  geometry of irregular singularities.
* **Naofumi Honda, Takahiro Kawai, and Yoshitsugu Takei, *Virtual
  Turning Points*** (2015, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-4-431-55702-9)). Exact WKB analysis and virtual
  turning points, in a short monograph.
* **Elena Shchepakina, Vladimir Sobolev, and Michael P. Mortell, *Singular
  Perturbations: Introduction to System Order Reduction Methods with
  Applications*** (2014,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-09570-7)). Slow manifolds,
  singular perturbations, and reduction of differential systems. A compact
  research account linking asymptotic expansions with dynamical-system
  geometry.
* **Robin Pemantle, Mark C. Wilson, and Stephen Melczer, *Analytic
  Combinatorics in Several Variables*** (2nd ed., 2024, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108874144)). Asymptotics of
  multivariate generating functions through residues, stratified Morse
  theory, and hyperbolic polynomials.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis, Sharp Spectral Asymptotics and
  Applications*** (5 volumes, 2019, Springer; [I](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30557-4), [II](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30541-3), [III](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30537-6), [IV](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30545-1), [V](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30561-1)). Listed in Section 30.
  Spectral asymptotics at the precision the classical Weyl law does not
  reach.

---

## 51. Integrable Systems and Riemann–Hilbert Methods

A small section, but the Riemann–Hilbert method has become a standard
analytic technique well beyond its origins. Beyond the classical inverse
scattering theory, the list covers algebro-geometric solitons, discrete
Painlevé equations, dispersive waves, boundary-value transforms, and
analytic and numerical Riemann–Hilbert methods.

### Foundational works

* **Percy Deift, *Orthogonal Polynomials and Random Matrices: A
  Riemann–Hilbert Approach***. The standard introduction to the
  nonlinear steepest-descent method.
* **Athanassios S. Fokas, Alexander R. Its, Andrei A. Kapaev, and
  Victor Yu. Novokshenov, *Painlevé Transcendents: The Riemann–Hilbert
  Approach***. The definitive reference for Painlevé asymptotics.
* **Vladimir E. Zakharov, Sergei V. Manakov, Sergei P. Novikov, and
  Lev P. Pitaevskii, *Theory of Solitons: The Inverse Scattering
  Method***. The founding treatise.
* **Mark J. Ablowitz and Athanassios S. Fokas, *Complex Variables:
  Introduction and Applications***. A complex-analysis course written
  by integrable-systems specialists, with Riemann–Hilbert problems
  treated from the start.
* **Ludwig D. Faddeev and Leon A. Takhtajan, *Hamiltonian Methods in
  the Theory of Solitons***. The Hamiltonian and spectral structure.
* **Olivier Babelon, Denis Bernard, and Michel Talon, *Introduction to
  Classical Integrable Systems*** (2003, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511535024)). Lax pairs, r-matrices,
  spectral curves, and their algebro-geometric and field-theoretic sides.
* **Vladimir A. Marchenko, *Sturm–Liouville Operators and
  Applications***. Inverse spectral theory, and the analytic core of
  the inverse scattering transform.
* **Fritz Gesztesy and Helge Holden, *Soliton Equations and Their
  Algebro-Geometric Solutions***, volume I, *(1+1)-Dimensional
  Continuous Models* (2003), and, with Johanna Michor and Gerald
  Teschl, volume II, *(1+1)-Dimensional Discrete Models* (2008,
  Cambridge University Press; [I](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511546723), [II](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/soliton-equations-and-their-algebrogeometric-solutions/444985FF5EB231A3101AEBE8DEDAFB2B)). Algebro-geometric solutions of soliton
  equations, with the spectral theory done carefully.
* **Spyridon Kamvissis, Kenneth D. T.-R. McLaughlin, and Peter D. Miller,
  *Semiclassical Soliton Ensembles for the Focusing Nonlinear Schrödinger
  Equation*** (2003, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400837182), Annals of Mathematics
  Studies). Nonlinear steepest descent in the semiclassical limit.
* **Jinho Baik, Thomas Kriecherbauer, Kenneth D. T.-R. McLaughlin, and Peter
  D. Miller, *Discrete Orthogonal Polynomials: Asymptotics and
  Applications*** (2007, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400837137), Annals of Mathematics
  Studies). Riemann–Hilbert analysis for discrete weights, with applications
  to random tilings.
* **Valerii I. Gromak, Ilpo Laine, and Shun Shimomura, *Painlevé
  Differential Equations in the Complex Plane*** (2002, [De Gruyter](https://doi.org/10.1515/9783110198096)).
  Listed in Section 41.
* **Robert Conte and Micheline Musette, *The Painlevé Handbook*** (2nd
  ed., 2020, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-53340-3)). Listed in Section 50.
* **Alexander I. Bobenko and Yuri B. Suris, *Discrete Differential Geometry:
  Integrable Structure*** (2008, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/098), Graduate Studies in
  Mathematics). Discrete surfaces and nets whose multidimensional
  consistency is the discrete form of integrability.
* **M. J. Ablowitz and P. A. Clarkson, *Solitons, Nonlinear Evolution
  Equations and Inverse Scattering***. Inverse scattering, Painlevé
  equations, and integrable systems.
* **C. Rogers and W. K. Schief, *Bäcklund and Darboux
  Transformations***. Bäcklund and Darboux transformations in geometry and
  soliton theory.
* **Morikazu Toda, *Theory of Nonlinear Lattices***. The Toda lattice,
  its solutions, and its integrability.

### More recent works

* **Mark J. Ablowitz, *Nonlinear Dispersive Waves: Asymptotic Analysis and
  Solitons*** (2011, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511998324)). Asymptotic derivation of
  dispersive models, solitons, and inverse scattering, with physical examples.
  A graduate route into integrable wave equations from their analytic origins.
* **Thomas Trogdon and Sheehan Olver, *Riemann–Hilbert Problems, Their
  Numerical Solution, and the Computation of Nonlinear Special Functions***
  (2015, [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611974201)). Analytic
  foundations of Riemann–Hilbert problems developed toward reliable
  computation. Included for its operator theory, asymptotic analysis, and
  nonlinear special functions.
* **Athanassios S. Fokas and Beatrice Pelloni (eds.), *Unified Transform for
  Boundary Value Problems: Applications and Advances*** (2014,
  [SIAM](https://doi.org/10.1137/1.9781611973822)). Substantial treatments of
  the unified transform for linear and integrable nonlinear boundary problems.
  Complements Fokas's earlier single-author introduction with subsequent
  developments.
* **Jinho Baik, Percy Deift, and Toufic Suidan, *Combinatorics and
  Random Matrix Theory*** (2016, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/172)). Listed
  in Section 7. The Riemann–Hilbert method in a combinatorial setting.
* **Dan Romik, *The Surprising Mathematics of Longest Increasing
  Subsequences*** (2015, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139872003)). Ulam's problem from the
  hook formula to the Baik–Deift–Johansson theorem and the Tracy–Widom law.
* **Nalini Joshi, *Discrete Painlevé Equations*** (2019, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/cbms/131), CBMS Regional Conference Series). Geometry,
  Lax pairs, and Riemann–Hilbert problems for discrete Painlevé
  equations.
* **Jarmo Hietarinta, Nalini Joshi, and Frank W. Nijhoff, *Discrete Systems
  and Integrability*** (2016, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781107337411)). Integrable difference
  equations, consistency around the cube, and discrete Painlevé equations.
* **Sergey M. Natanzon, *Complex Analysis, Riemann Surfaces and Integrable
  Systems*** (2019, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-34640-9)).
  Riemann surfaces, theta functions, and algebro-geometric methods for
  integrable equations. A graduate bridge between complex geometry and the
  analytic theory of integrability.
* **David Lannes, *The Water Waves Problem*** (2013, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/188)). Listed in Section 34. Hamiltonian PDE at the
  edge of the integrable theory.

---

# Part IX. Global and Geometric Analysis

---

## 52. Geometric Analysis on Manifolds

Analysis shaped by Riemannian geometry: comparison theory and heat kernels,
nonlinear elliptic problems and harmonic maps, Ricci and mean curvature
flow, and minimal surfaces. The more recent books add Ricci-flow and
mean-curvature-flow monographs, the sub-Riemannian treatise, symplectic and
gauge-theoretic analysis, and mathematical relativity.

### Foundational works

#### Riemannian geometry, comparison, and heat kernels

* **John M. Lee, *Introduction to Riemannian Manifolds*** (2nd ed., 2018,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-91755-9), Graduate Texts in
  Mathematics; first edition 1997). The standard first course in Riemannian
  geometry.
* **Jürgen Jost, *Riemannian Geometry and Geometric Analysis***. The
  standard graduate bridge from Riemannian geometry to harmonic maps,
  variational problems, and elliptic estimates.
* **Isaac Chavel, *Riemannian Geometry: A Modern
  Introduction***. Geometric foundations with substantial analytic
  content.
* **Peter Petersen, *Riemannian Geometry***. Primarily geometry, but
  the standard reference for the comparison theory that feeds into
  analysis.
* **Jeff Cheeger and David G. Ebin, *Comparison Theorems in Riemannian
  Geometry***. Short, foundational, and still the cleanest account of
  comparison arguments.
* **Isaac Chavel, *Eigenvalues in Riemannian Geometry***. The classic
  reference for spectral geometry and geometric eigenvalue
  inequalities.
* **Richard Schoen and Shing-Tung Yau, *Lectures on Differential
  Geometry***. Research-level geometry shaped throughout by PDE,
  curvature, and minimal-surface methods.
* **Alexander Grigor'yan, *Heat Kernel and Analysis on
  Manifolds***. Heat kernels, volume growth, parabolic estimates, and
  the analytic consequences of geometry.
* **Jürgen Jost, *Partial Differential Equations*** and ***Nonpositive
  Curvature: Geometric and Analytic Aspects***. The analytic
  companions to Jost's geometry.
* **Luca Capogna, Donatella Danielli, Scott D. Pauls, and Jeremy T.
  Tyson, *An Introduction to the Heisenberg Group and the
  Sub-Riemannian Isoperimetric Problem*** (2007, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-7643-8133-2)). The model
  case of the sub-Riemannian isoperimetric problem.

#### Nonlinear elliptic problems and harmonic maps

* **Thierry Aubin, *Some Nonlinear Problems in Riemannian Geometry***
  and ***Nonlinear Analysis on Manifolds: Monge–Ampère
  Equations***. Sobolev inequalities, Yamabe-type problems, and
  complex Monge–Ampère on manifolds.
* **Emmanuel Hebey, *Nonlinear Analysis on Manifolds: Sobolev Spaces
  and Inequalities***. The functional-analytic complement to Aubin,
  careful about best constants.
* **Olivier Druet, Emmanuel Hebey, and Frédéric Robert, *Blow-up Theory
  for Elliptic PDEs in Riemannian Geometry*** (2004, [Princeton
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400826162), Annals of Mathematics Studies). Blow-up analysis
  for critical elliptic equations on manifolds.
* **Sun-Yung Alice Chang, *Non-linear Elliptic Equations in Conformal
  Geometry*** (2004, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/006), Zurich Lectures in Advanced
  Mathematics). Conformally covariant operators, Q-curvature, and extremal
  metrics.
* **Fanghua Lin and Changyou Wang, *The Analysis of Harmonic Maps and
  Their Heat Flows***. Harmonic maps, partial regularity,
  singularities, and evolution.
* **Frédéric Hélein, *Harmonic Maps, Conservation Laws and Moving
  Frames***. Compensated compactness and regularity for harmonic maps.

#### Geometric flows

* **Peter Topping, *Lectures on the Ricci Flow***. Short, clear, and
  the usual first exposure.
* **Bennett Chow, Peng Lu, and Lei Ni, *Hamilton's Ricci Flow***. The
  detailed reference for Ricci flow as a partial differential
  equation.
* **Bennett Chow, Sun-Chin Chu, David Glickenstein, Christine Guenther,
  James Isenberg, Tom Ivey, Dan Knopf, Peng Lu, Feng Luo, and Lei Ni,
  *The Ricci Flow: Techniques and Applications*** (several volumes,
  2007–2015, American Mathematical Society; [I](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/135), [II](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/144), [III](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/163), [IV](https://doi.org/10.1090/surv/206)). The technical companion to
  *Hamilton's Ricci Flow*.
* **John Morgan and Gang Tian, *Ricci Flow and the Poincaré
  Conjecture*** (2007, [American Mathematical Society](https://www.claymath.org/resource/ricci-flow-and-the-poincare-conjecture/)) and ***The
  Geometrization Conjecture*** (2014, [American Mathematical Society](https://www.claymath.org/resource/the-geometrization-conjecture/)).
  The detailed exposition of Perelman's proof.
* **Klaus Ecker, *Regularity Theory for Mean Curvature Flow***. The
  research monograph on regularity for mean curvature flow.

#### Minimal surfaces

* **Robert Osserman, *A Survey of Minimal Surfaces***. A classical
  compact survey of enduring value.
* **H. Blaine Lawson, Jr., *Lectures on Minimal Submanifolds***. A
  classical geometric treatment.
* **Ulrich Dierkes, Stefan Hildebrandt, and Friedrich Sauvigny,
  *Minimal Surfaces*** and companion volumes. Broad PDE and regularity
  coverage of the Plateau problem.
* **Michael Struwe, *Plateau's Problem and the Calculus of
  Variations***. Short and focused on the variational analysis.

#### Symplectic, gauge-theoretic, and Lorentzian analysis

* **Dusa McDuff and Dietmar Salamon, *Introduction to Symplectic Topology***
  (3rd ed., 2017, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198794899.001.0001); first edition
  1995). Symplectic manifolds, capacities, and Floer-theoretic ideas, with
  the analytic background. The companion to their *J-holomorphic Curves*.
* **Dusa McDuff and Dietmar Salamon, *J-holomorphic Curves and
  Symplectic Topology*** (2nd ed., 2012, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/coll/052);
  first edition 2004). Pseudoholomorphic curves. Included because the
  analysis — elliptic regularity, bubbling, and transversality — is the
  content analysts use.
* **Liviu I. Nicolaescu, *An Invitation to Morse Theory*** (2nd ed.,
  2011, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-1105-5); first edition
  2007). Morse theory with the analytic transversality made explicit.
* **Peter Kronheimer and Tomasz Mrowka, *Monopoles and
  Three-Manifolds*** (2007, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511543111)). Seiberg–Witten
  monopoles and the analytic estimates behind the topological
  applications.
* **Katrin Wehrheim, *Uhlenbeck Compactness*** (2004, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/004)). Complete proofs of Uhlenbeck's
  gauge-fixing and compactness theorems.
* **Christian Bär, Nicolas Ginoux, and Frank Pfäffle, *Wave Equations on
  Lorentzian Manifolds and Quantization*** (2007, [European Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/037)). Fundamental solutions and Green
  operators for normally hyperbolic operators on globally hyperbolic
  spacetimes.

### More recent works

#### Riemannian, sub-Riemannian, and conformal analysis

* **Peter Li, *Geometric Analysis*** (2012, Cambridge University
  Press). Gradient estimates, harmonic functions, eigenvalue bounds,
  and analysis on complete manifolds.
* **Andrei Agrachev, Davide Barilari, and Ugo Boscain, *A Comprehensive
  Introduction to Sub-Riemannian Geometry*** (2020, [Cambridge
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108677325)). The current treatise on sub-Riemannian geometry,
  from geodesics to the heat equation.
* **Emmanuel Hebey, *Compactness and Stability for Nonlinear Elliptic
  Equations*** (2014, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/134), Zurich Lectures in
  Advanced Mathematics). Compactness and stability of the Yamabe-type
  equations, in a research course.
* **Gábor Székelyhidi, *An Introduction to Extremal Kähler Metrics***
  (2014, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/152)). Listed in Section 45.
* **Dan A. Lee, *Geometric Relativity*** (2019, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/201), Graduate Studies in
  Mathematics). Positive mass theorems, Penrose inequalities, and the
  constraint equations, treated as geometric analysis.

#### Geometric flows

* **Ben Andrews and Christopher Hopper, *The Ricci Flow in Riemannian
  Geometry*** (2011, Springer, Lecture Notes in Mathematics). A concise
  geometric introduction to Ricci flow.
* **Simon Brendle, *Ricci Flow and the Sphere Theorem*** (2010,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/111), Graduate Studies in Mathematics). The
  differentiable sphere theorem and the Ricci-flow arguments around it.
* **Bennett Chow, *Ricci Solitons in Low Dimensions*** (2023, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://www.ams.org/books/gsm/235/)). Ricci solitons
  in dimensions two and three and their role in Ricci-flow singularities. A
  focused graduate introduction before the broader research literature on
  geometric evolution.
* **Carlo Mantegazza, *Lecture Notes on Mean Curvature Flow*** (2011,
  Birkhäuser, Progress in Mathematics). An accessible analytic
  treatment of mean curvature flow.
* **Ben Andrews, Bennett Chow, Christine Guenther, and Mat Langford,
  *Extrinsic Geometric Flows*** (2020, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/206),
  Graduate Studies in Mathematics). Curve shortening and mean curvature
  flow, as a graduate treatise.

#### Minimal surfaces

* **Tobias H. Colding and William P. Minicozzi II, *A Course in
  Minimal Surfaces*** (2011, American Mathematical Society, Graduate
  Studies in Mathematics). The modern analytic theory of minimal
  surfaces.
* **William H. Meeks III and Joaquín Pérez, *A Survey on Classical
  Minimal Surface Theory*** (2012, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/ulect/060),
  University Lecture Series). The modern survey of classical minimal
  surface theory after Osserman and Lawson.
* **Antonio Alarcón, Franc Forstnerič, and Francisco J. López, *Minimal
  Surfaces from a Complex Analytic Viewpoint*** (2021, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-69056-4)).
  Listed in Section 45.

#### Symplectic and gauge-theoretic analysis

* **Michèle Audin and Mihai Damian, *Morse Theory and Floer Homology***
  (2014, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4471-5496-9),
  Universitext). Morse homology and a complete construction of Hamiltonian
  Floer homology, with the analysis done in detail.
* **Leonid Polterovich and Daniel Rosen, *Function Theory on Symplectic
  Manifolds*** (2014, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/books/crmm/034/)). Poisson brackets,
  quasi-states, and rigidity of functions on symplectic manifolds. Included
  for its functional-analytic and variational viewpoint on symplectic
  geometry.
* **Kai Cieliebak and Yakov Eliashberg, *From Stein to Weinstein and Back:
  Symplectic Geometry of Affine Complex Manifolds*** (2012, [American
  Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/coll/059), Colloquium
  Publications). Stein manifolds, plurisubharmonic functions, and Weinstein
  structures. Also relevant to Section 45.

---

## 53. Elliptic Operators on Manifolds, Index Theory, and Spectral Geometry

Elliptic theory and Hodge theory on manifolds, the index theorem and Dirac
operators, and the spectral geometry of the Laplacian. The more recent books
treat higher index theory, Bergman kernels, hyperbolic surfaces, Dirac
operators, and L²-invariants.

### Foundational works

#### Elliptic operators, Hodge theory, and partial differential relations

* **Michael E. Taylor, *Partial Differential Equations I–II***. The
  most accessible route into elliptic theory on manifolds and Hodge
  theory for an analyst.
* **Frank W. Warner, *Foundations of Differentiable Manifolds and Lie
  Groups***. The standard background, including a careful treatment of
  the Hodge theorem.
* **Liviu I. Nicolaescu, *Lectures on the Geometry of Manifolds***. A
  large modern course covering elliptic operators, Hodge theory, and
  index theory in one place.
* **M. A. Shubin, *Pseudodifferential Operators and Spectral
  Theory***. Global symbol calculus and spectral asymptotics.
* **Mikhail Gromov, *Partial Differential Relations***. Difficult and
  unclassifiable, but the origin of the h-principle and of much modern
  flexibility theory.

#### Index theory and Dirac operators

* **John Roe, *Elliptic Operators, Topology and Asymptotic
  Methods***. The most readable book-length account of index theory
  for analysts.
* **Peter B. Gilkey, *Invariance Theory, the Heat Equation, and the
  Atiyah–Singer Index Theorem***. The heat-kernel proof, done
  thoroughly.
* **Nicole Berline, Ezra Getzler, and Michèle Vergne, *Heat Kernels
  and Dirac Operators***. The standard reference for the local index
  theorem and its supersymmetric proof.
* **H. Blaine Lawson, Jr. and Marie-Louise Michelsohn, *Spin
  Geometry***. The comprehensive reference for Dirac operators and
  their analysis.
* **Thomas Friedrich, *Dirac Operators in Riemannian Geometry*** (2000,
  [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/025), Graduate
  Studies in Mathematics). Spin geometry, Dirac operators, and eigenvalue
  estimates.
* **Bernhelm Booss-Bavnbek and Krzysztof P. Wojciechowski, *Elliptic
  Boundary Problems for Dirac Operators***. Boundary value problems
  and spectral flow.
* **Richard B. Melrose, *The Atiyah–Patodi–Singer Index Theorem*** and
  ***Geometric Scattering Theory***. Microlocal analysis on manifolds
  with boundary and with ends.
* **Nigel Higson and John Roe, *Analytic K-Homology*** (2000, [Oxford
  University Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780198511762.001.0001)). Listed in Section 23.
* **Wolfgang Lück, *L²-Invariants: Theory and Applications to Geometry and
  K-Theory*** (2002, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-662-04687-6),
  Ergebnisse). L²-Betti numbers, L²-torsion, and the Atiyah conjecture: von
  Neumann algebras applied to topology.
* **Xiaonan Ma and George Marinescu, *Holomorphic Morse Inequalities and
  Bergman Kernels*** (2007, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-7643-8115-8)). Bergman kernel asymptotics and
  holomorphic Morse inequalities.
* **Jean-Michel Bismut and Gilles Lebeau, *The Hypoelliptic Laplacian
  and Ray–Singer Metrics*** (2008, [Princeton University Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400829064), Annals
  of Mathematics Studies). The hypoelliptic Laplacian and its relation
  to the Ray–Singer torsion.

#### Spectral geometry

* **Isaac Chavel, *Eigenvalues in Riemannian Geometry***. The classic
  reference for eigenvalues and the geometry of the Laplacian.
* **Pierre H. Bérard, *Spectral Geometry: Direct and Inverse
  Problems***. Direct and inverse spectral geometry in the classical
  style.
* **Peter B. Gilkey, *Asymptotic Formulae in Spectral Geometry*** (2004,
  [CRC Press](https://doi.org/10.1201/9780203490464)). Heat-trace and
  eta-invariant asymptotics, with boundary conditions.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis and Precise Spectral
  Asymptotics***. The definitive treatment of sharp Weyl laws.
* **Christopher D. Sogge, *Hangzhou Lectures on Eigenfunctions of the
  Laplacian*** and ***Fourier Integrals in Classical
  Analysis***. Harmonic-analytic eigenfunction estimates.
* **Henryk Iwaniec, *Spectral Methods of Automorphic Forms*** (2nd ed.,
  2002, [American Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.1090/gsm/053),
  Graduate Studies in Mathematics; first edition 1995). Spectral theory of
  the Laplacian on hyperbolic surfaces, Eisenstein series, and the Selberg
  trace formula.
* **David Borthwick, *Spectral Theory of Infinite-Area Hyperbolic
  Surfaces*** (2nd ed., 2016, [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-33877-4); first edition 2007). Listed in Section 22.
* **Antoine Henrot, *Extremum Problems for Eigenvalues of Elliptic
  Operators***. The extremal side of spectral geometry.
* **Antoine Henrot and Michel Pierre, *Shape Variation and
  Optimization*** (2018, [European Mathematical Society](https://doi.org/10.4171/178); French original
  2005). Listed in Section 36. Extremal eigenvalues as a
  shape-optimisation problem.
* **Steven Rosenberg, *The Laplacian on a Riemannian Manifold***. An
  introduction to Hodge theory, heat kernels, and the heat-equation proof
  of the Chern–Gauss–Bonnet theorem.

### More recent works

* **Michael Levitin, Dan Mangoubi, and Iosif Polterovich, *Topics in Spectral
  Geometry*** (2023, [American Mathematical
  Society](https://www.ams.org/publications/authors/books/postpub/gsm-237)).
  Eigenvalues, eigenfunctions, variational principles, and the geometry of the
  Laplacian. A modern graduate course complementing the classical manifold and
  index-theory references.
* **Nicolas Bergeron, *The Spectrum of Hyperbolic Surfaces*** (2016,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-27666-3)). Selberg, geodesic flows, and the spectrum of hyperbolic
  surfaces, as a graduate text.
* **Steve Zelditch, *Eigenfunctions of the Laplacian on a Riemannian
  Manifold*** (2017, American Mathematical Society, CBMS Regional
  Conference Series). The modern survey of eigenfunction concentration,
  quantum ergodicity, and nodal sets.
* **Victor Ivrii, *Microlocal Analysis, Sharp Spectral Asymptotics and
  Applications*** (5 volumes, 2019, Springer; [I](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30557-4), [II](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30541-3), [III](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30537-6), [IV](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30545-1), [V](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-30561-1)). Listed in Section 30.
* **Gabriel P. Paternain, Mikko Salo, and Gunther Uhlmann, *Geometric
  Inverse Problems*** (2023, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009039901)).
  Listed in Section 22. The inverse spectral direction, complementing
  Zelditch's eigenfunction book.
* **David D. Bleecker and Bernhelm Booss-Bavnbek, *[Index Theory with
  Applications to Mathematics and Physics](https://thiele.ruc.dk/~booss/A-S-Index-Book/Index_12_01.htm)*** (2013, International Press).
  The index theorem through several proofs and many examples.
* **Rufus Willett and Guoliang Yu, *Higher Index Theory*** (2020,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108867351)). Listed in Section 23.

---

# Part X. The Working Apparatus

---

## 54. Problem Books, Counterexamples, and Reference Tables

These are not substitutes for systematic texts. They are the books
analysts reach for when they need an example, a counterexample, a
classical trick, or a formula. The more recent additions are
specialised counterexample and problem collections, together with
handbooks and catalogues of formulae.

### Foundational works

* **G. Pólya and G. Szegő, *Problems and Theorems in Analysis
  I–II***. The most important classical problem collection in
  analysis; still capable of teaching techniques not found in
  textbooks.
* **B. M. Makarov, M. G. Goluzina, A. A. Lodkin, and A. N. Podkorytov,
  *Selected Problems in Real Analysis***. Genuinely difficult problems
  in real-variable analysis and measure theory.
* **W. J. Kaczor and M. T. Nowak, *Problems in Mathematical Analysis
  I–III***. A large graded collection with full solutions.
* **Teodora-Liliana Rădulescu, Vicenţiu D. Rădulescu, and Titu Andreescu,
  *Problems in Real Analysis: Advanced Calculus on the Real Axis*** (2009,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-77379-7)). Problems on
  sequences, series, continuity, differentiability, and integration, with
  solutions.
* **Charalambos D. Aliprantis and Owen Burkinshaw, *Problems in Real
  Analysis***. The companion to their *Principles of Real Analysis*,
  and a good source of routine-but-instructive exercises.
* **Paul R. Halmos, *A Hilbert Space Problem Book***. Operator theory
  and Hilbert space taught entirely through problems; still without
  equal.
* **Konrad Knopp, *Problem Book in the Theory of Functions
  I–II***. The classical complex-analysis problem collection.
* **Albert N. Shiryaev, *Problems in Probability***, translated by
  Andrew Lyasoff (2012, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4614-3688-1)). A large problem collection across
  graduate probability, with solutions.
* **Bernard R. Gelbaum and John M. H. Olmsted, *Counterexamples in
  Analysis***. The standard compact source of pathology.
* **Lynn Arthur Steen and J. Arthur Seebach, Jr., *Counterexamples in
  Topology***. Its analytic uses are more frequent than one expects.
* **Jordan M. Stoyanov, *Counterexamples in Probability*** (3rd ed., 2013,
  [Dover](https://store.doverpublications.com/products/9780486315935)). The standard counterexample collection for probability.
* **Ralph P. Boas, Jr., *A Primer of Real Functions***. Short,
  example-driven, and full of useful pathology.
* **J. Michael Steele, *The Cauchy–Schwarz Master Class***. The best
  modern source for inequality technique as a craft.
* **Walter Rudin, *Real and Complex Analysis*** and ***Functional
  Analysis***. Their exercises remain part of the informal graduate
  canon.
* **The Bateman Manuscript Project volumes**. Still the place for a
  classical special-function identity when the DLMF does not have it.
* **I. S. Gradshteyn and I. M. Ryzhik, *Table of Integrals, Series,
  and Products***. The standard integral table for a classical
  identity the DLMF does not record.
* **A. P. Prudnikov, Yu. A. Brychkov, and O. I. Marichev, *Integrals
  and Series***. The most complete integral and transform tables.
* **Andrei N. Borodin and Paavo Salminen, *Handbook of Brownian Motion —
  Facts and Formulae*** (2nd ed., 2002,
  [Birkhäuser](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-0348-8163-0)). Distributions of
  functionals of Brownian motion and related diffusions, in tables. Used
  rather than read.
* **Encyclopedia of Mathematics (Springer/EMS, online)** and **Wolfram
  Functions Site**. Useful for orientation and for formula checking
  respectively; both to be verified against a primary source.

### More recent works

* **Barry Simon, *A Comprehensive Course in Analysis*** (2015, American
  Mathematical Society). The problem sets and notes make the series a
  standing reference beyond its exposition.
* **René L. Schilling and Franziska Kühn, *Counterexamples in Measure
  and Integration*** (2021, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009003797)). Listed in
  Section 4.
* **Stephan Ramon Garcia, Javad Mashreghi, and William T. Ross,
  *Operator Theory by Example*** (2023, [Oxford University Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/oso/9780192863867.001.0001)).
  Listed in Section 21. Operator theory taught by worked examples.
* **Paolo Baldi, *Stochastic Calculus*** (2017, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-62226-2)). Listed in
  Section 8. A stochastic-calculus course whose exercises are half the
  book.
* **W. K. Hayman and Eleanor F. Lingham, *Research Problems in Function
  Theory*** (2019, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-25165-9)). Listed in Section 41. Open problems, not
  exercises, but the list analysts in classical function theory use.
* **[NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions](https://dlmf.nist.gov/)** (2010, NIST). For special
  functions, now the first reference to consult, ahead of the older
  tables.
* **Roelof Koekoek, Peter A. Lesky, and René F. Swarttouw,
  *Hypergeometric Orthogonal Polynomials and Their q-Analogues***
  (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-05014-5)). Listed in Section 48. The catalogue of
  formulae for the Askey scheme, corresponding to the DLMF for special
  functions.

---

## 55. Foundations, Constructive Analysis, and Machine-Formalized Mathematics

Formalized mathematics has reached the point where a graduate analyst
may encounter it as a research tool, a teaching instrument, or a
collaborator's working environment, and the constructive and
reverse-mathematical literatures are the natural bridge to it. Beside
the classical texts, the section covers constructive analysis and
probability, algorithmic randomness, computable analysis, proof
mining, reverse mathematics, the current Lean and Rocq textbooks,
validated numerics, and type-theoretic foundations.

### Foundational works

* **Errett Bishop and Douglas Bridges, *Constructive Analysis***. The
  foundational text of Bishop-style constructive mathematics; a
  genuine development of analysis, not a philosophical tract.
* **Douglas S. Bridges and Luminiţa Simona Vîţă, *Techniques of
  Constructive Analysis***. A modern, readable continuation.
* **Anne Sjerp Troelstra and Dirk van Dalen, *Constructivism in
  Mathematics I–II***. The systematic logical background.
* **Stephen G. Simpson, *Subsystems of Second Order Arithmetic***. The
  standard reference for reverse mathematics, and the best account of
  exactly which axioms the standard theorems of analysis require.
* **Klaus Weihrauch, *Computable Analysis***. The type-two theory of
  effectivity; the standard framework for computability over the
  reals.
* **Marian B. Pour-El and J. Ian Richards, *Computability in Analysis
  and Physics***. The classical account of computable and
  noncomputable phenomena in analysis.
* **André Nies, *Computability and Randomness*** (2009, [Oxford University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1093/acprof:oso/9780199230761.001.0001)).
  Algorithmic randomness and lowness notions; the companion to
  Downey–Hirschfeldt.
* **Ulrich Kohlenbach, *Applied Proof Theory: Proof Interpretations and
  their Use in Mathematics*** (2008, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-540-77533-1)). Proof mining and the
  extraction of quantitative bounds from analytic proofs.
* **Abraham Robinson, *Non-standard Analysis***. The founding account
  of nonstandard analysis.
* **Albert E. Hurd and Peter A. Loeb, *An Introduction to Nonstandard
  Real Analysis***. Nonstandard real analysis and the Loeb measure
  construction, with applications in probability and functional
  analysis.
* **Peter A. Loeb and Manfred P. H. Wolff (eds.), *Nonstandard Analysis for
  the Working Mathematician*** (2nd ed., 2015,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-94-017-7327-0)). Nonstandard
  methods in measure theory, functional analysis, probability, and
  economics.
* **Sergio Albeverio, Jens Erik Fenstad, Raphael Høegh-Krohn, and Tom
  Lindstrøm, *Nonstandard Methods in Stochastic Analysis and
  Mathematical Physics***. Where the nonstandard machinery earned its
  place.
* **John Harrison, *Handbook of Practical Logic and Automated Reasoning***
  (2009, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511576430)). Decision procedures,
  first-order automation, and interactive proof, by the author of the HOL
  Light real-analysis library.
* **Freek Wiedijk (ed.), *The Seventeen Provers of the
  World***. Dated, but still the clearest comparative orientation.
* **Nicolas Bourbaki, *Theory of Sets***. Included here rather than in
  Part I: the Bourbaki treatment of structure, filters, and uniform
  spaces is the closest classical antecedent to how a proof assistant
  organizes analysis.
* **Robert Goldblatt, *Lectures on the Hyperreals: An Introduction to
  Nonstandard Analysis***. The ultrapower construction of the hyperreals
  and nonstandard analysis.

### More recent works

#### Constructive mathematics, computability, and reverse mathematics

* **Douglas Bridges, Hajime Ishihara, Michael Rathjen, and Helmut
  Schwichtenberg (eds.), *Handbook of Constructive Mathematics*** (2023,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781009039888)). A
  substantial handbook of constructive analysis, topology, foundations, and
  computation. Supplies a modern research panorama beyond the Bishop–Bridges
  and Troelstra–van Dalen books.
* **Yuen-Kwok Chan, *Foundations of Constructive Probability Theory*** (2021,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108884013)).
  Probability developed constructively, with explicit attention to the
  information needed to establish convergence and existence. A specialised
  continuation of Bishop-style analysis.
* **Denis R. Hirschfeldt, *Slicing the Truth*** (2014, World Scientific).
  A shorter and more current introduction to the reverse-mathematics
  programme.
* **Damir D. Dzhafarov and Carl Mummert, *Reverse Mathematics: Problems,
  Reductions, and Proofs*** (2022,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-11367-3)). Modern reverse
  mathematics, with computable reducibilities between problems.
* **Vasco Brattka and Peter Hertling (eds.), *Handbook of Computability
  and Complexity in Analysis*** (2021, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-59234-9)). The research handbook
  of computable analysis: computable reals, computable functionals, and
  the complexity of analytic operations.
* **Rodney G. Downey and Denis R. Hirschfeldt, *Algorithmic Randomness and
  Complexity*** (2010, [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-0-387-68441-3)).
  A major research reference on effective randomness and its connections with
  computability and measure. Supplies the foundational background for
  effective probabilistic analysis.
* **Johanna N. Y. Franklin and Christopher P. Porter (eds.), *Algorithmic
  Randomness: Progress and Prospects*** (2020, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108781718)). Research surveys on
  effective randomness, computability, and connections with analysis. A later
  continuation of the Downey–Hirschfeldt monograph.
* **Helmut Schwichtenberg and Stanley S. Wainer, *Proofs and
  Computations*** (2012, [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139031905)). Primitive
  recursion, computability, and the proof theory that constructive
  analysis uses.

#### Logic, type theory, and proof assistants

* **Jeremy Avigad, *Mathematical Logic and Computation*** (2022,
  [Cambridge University Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/9781108778756)). A
  modern logic course written with formalization in mind.
* **Rob Nederpelt and Herman Geuvers, *Type Theory and Formal Proof: An
  Introduction*** (2014, [Cambridge University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9781139567725)). The lambda cube and the
  calculus of constructions as the foundations of proof assistants.
* **The Univalent Foundations Program, *Homotopy Type Theory: Univalent
  Foundations of Mathematics*** (2013,
  [online](https://homotopytypetheory.org/book/)). Type theory with the
  univalence axiom, including a chapter on constructions of the real
  numbers.
* **Jeremy Avigad, Leonardo de Moura, Soonho Kong, and Sebastian
  Ullrich, *Theorem Proving in Lean***, and **Jeremy Avigad and
  Patrick Massot, *Mathematics in Lean*** (online). The standard
  introductions to the Lean proof assistant and its mathematical library.
* **Anne Baanen, Alexander Bentkamp, Jasmin Blanchette, Xavier
  Généreux, Johannes Hölzl, and Jannis Limperg, *[The Hitchhiker's Guide
  to Logical Verification](https://github.com/lean-forward/logical_verification_2026)*** (living textbook, Lean 4; the 2026 edition
  is the current one). An undergraduate-to-graduate introduction to
  interactive theorem proving in Lean, with the mathematics developed
  formally.
* **The Mathlib library and its documentation**. Now the largest
  coherent formalization of analysis in existence, covering measure
  theory, functional analysis, one-variable complex analysis, Fourier
  analysis, and parts of PDE; effectively a reference work in its own
  right, and one that states hypotheses with a precision no textbook
  matches.
* **Assia Mahboubi and Enrico Tassi, *[Mathematical Components](https://math-comp.github.io/mcb/)***
  (online book, with the MathComp library). The working introduction to
  the Mathematical Components library in Rocq, the main ecosystem
  besides Lean.
* **The Coq/Rocq analysis libraries (Coquelicot, MathComp-Analysis)**.
  The main alternative ecosystem, with a different treatment of limits and
  filters.

#### Validated numerics and computer-assisted proof

* **Warwick Tucker, *Validated Numerics: A Short Introduction to Rigorous
  Computations*** (2011, [Princeton University
  Press](https://doi.org/10.1515/9781400838974)). Interval arithmetic and
  computer-assisted proofs in analysis and dynamics.
* **Mitsuhiro T. Nakao, Michael Plum, and Yoshitaka Watanabe, *Numerical
  Verification Methods and Computer-Assisted Proofs for Partial Differential
  Equations*** (2019,
  [Springer](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-981-13-7669-6)). Computer-assisted
  existence proofs for elliptic and parabolic problems.

---

# Suggested Reading Paths

No analyst reads a bibliography linearly. The more useful way to
interpret "core knowledge" is by layers.

## A. First graduate year: a common foundation

1. **Real analysis and measure**: Folland, Rudin, Royden–Fitzpatrick,
   or Stein–Shakarchi *Real Analysis*.
2. **Functional analysis**: Conway, Rudin, Brezis, or Stein–Shakarchi
   *Functional Analysis*.
3. **Complex analysis**: Ahlfors, Conway, Stein–Shakarchi, Gamelin, or
   Remmert.
4. **PDE**: Evans, Taylor I, Folland, or Renardy–Rogers.
5. **Probability, where it is part of the programme**: Billingsley,
   Durrett, Williams, or Kallenberg used selectively.

By the end of this stage one should be fluent in distributions, weak
convergence, Sobolev spaces, the Fourier transform, and basic spectral
theory, whether or not any course was labelled with those names.

## B. Second-course infrastructure

The books that most change how a graduate student actually proves
things:

- Stein, *Singular Integrals*, and Grafakos I;
- Adams–Fournier or Leoni on Sobolev spaces;
- Bergh–Löfström on interpolation;
- Hörmander I on distributions and Fourier analysis;
- Kato on operators, and Reed–Simon I–II;
- Engel–Nagel or Pazy on semigroups;
- Gilbarg–Trudinger on elliptic PDE;
- Evans–Gariepy on fine properties;
- Rockafellar on convex analysis, and Bauschke–Combettes on monotone
  operators;
- Kallenberg or Durrett for probability, with Ledoux–Talagrand or
  Boucheron–Lugosi–Massart for concentration;
- Villani, *Topics in Optimal Transportation*, which has become a
  common second language.

## C. Research entry by direction

**Harmonic analysis.** Stein's two monographs and Grafakos I–II, then
Muscalu–Schlag, Cruz-Uribe–Martell–Pérez for the
sparse and weighted theory, Demeter for decoupling, and Wolff or
Mattila for the geometric side.

**Linear and nonlinear PDE.** Hörmander I–IV; Gilbarg–Trudinger,
Ladyzhenskaya–Ural'tseva, and Krylov for elliptic and parabolic
regularity; Caffarelli–Cabré and Crandall–Ishii–Lions for the fully
nonlinear theory; Dafermos and Bressan for conservation laws; Tao,
Cazenave, and Bahouri–Chemin–Danchin for dispersive equations.

**Functional and operator analysis.** Kato, Reed–Simon,
Dunford–Schwartz; Sz.-Nagy–Foiaş and Nikolski for model theory;
Takesaki and Kadison–Ringrose for operator algebras;
Lindenstrauss–Tzafriri, Pisier, and Albiac–Kalton for Banach spaces;
Hytönen–van Neerven–Veraar–Weis for the vector-valued theory.

**Geometric measure theory and variational analysis.** Maggi and Simon
first, then Federer as a reference; Ambrosio–Fusco–Pallara for BV;
Dacorogna and Dal Maso for variational convergence;
Ambrosio–Gigli–Savaré and Villani for transport and gradient flows;
David–Semmes and Tolsa for quantitative rectifiability.

**Complex analysis, one variable.** Burckel or Remmert for the
classical theory in full; Garnett and Duren for Hardy spaces;
Pommerenke and Garnett–Marshall for boundary behaviour and harmonic
measure; Levin and Koosis for entire functions and the logarithmic
integral; Nikolski for the operator-theoretic direction.

**Complex analysis, several variables.** Hörmander and Range first;
then Chen–Shaw and Straube for the d-bar-Neumann problem; Demailly and
Forstnerič for the geometric and Oka-theoretic directions; Klimek and
Guedj–Zeriahi for pluripotential theory; Baouendi–Ebenfelt–Rothschild
for CR mappings.

**Probability and stochastic analysis.** Kallenberg, Revuz–Yor,
Rogers–Williams, Ethier–Kurtz, Stroock–Varadhan; then Nualart for
Malliavin calculus, Friz–Hairer for rough paths, and Da Prato–Zabczyk
for SPDE.

**Special functions and asymptotics.** Andrews–Askey–Roy and the DLMF;
Olver and Wong for asymptotics; Ismail and Simon for orthogonal
polynomials; Costin or Balser for summability; Deift for the
Riemann–Hilbert method.

**Spectral theory and mathematical physics.** Reed–Simon and Kato;
Teschl or Schmüdgen for a modern foundation; Zworski and
Dimassi–Sjöstrand for the semiclassical theory; Dyatlov–Zworski for
resonances.

## D. A note on how to use a list like this

Most of these books will never be read completely by anyone, and that
is not a defect. The practical skill is knowing which book a given
question belongs to. It is worth spending an hour with the table of
contents of a major reference before needing it, so that later the
memory to be retrieved is "this is a Maz'ya question" or "this is a
Burckel question" rather than a search.

---

# What This Bibliography Deliberately Does Not Try to Do

- **Neighbouring fields appear only where they support analytic
  practice.** Algebraic geometry, differential topology,
  representation theory, and logic are represented by the specific
  books analysts actually open.
- **Numerical analysis and scientific computing are largely omitted**,
  despite their deep interaction with approximation theory, PDE, and
  spectral theory. A few exceptions are admitted where the book is the
  best available account of a piece of analysis (Trefethen, de Boor,
  LeVeque, Peyré–Cuturi).
- **Mathematical statistics is omitted** except where it coincides
  with probability, empirical processes, or asymptotics.
- **Analytic number theory is omitted as a subject**, although many of
  its tools are harmonic or complex analytic; Montgomery's *Ten
  Lectures* is included precisely because it sits on the
  boundary. Readers wanting the subject itself should begin with
  Iwaniec–Kowalski.
- **Mathematical physics is included only where the analysis is the
  point** — spectral theory, scattering, statistical mechanics of
  operator algebras, kinetic theory — and not otherwise.
- **Specialized literatures in stochastic PDE, random matrices,
  inverse problems, integrable systems, and free probability are
  represented by their nearest stable monographs** rather than by
  article lists, even where the research front is entirely in papers.
- **Research areas whose canonical literature is still primarily
  periodical** are represented by lecture notes and surveys where
  these have stabilized, and are otherwise omitted rather than guessed
  at.
- **No claim of completeness or of ranking is made.** Several
  excellent books are absent because they never became common
  reference points, and a few unfashionable books are present because
  they still are.

The operative test throughout: a book belongs here if knowing that it
exists, and roughly what role it plays, is part of the working culture
of graduate or research-level analysis.
